7.4 From Graphs to FO Semiring Provenance
Given a formula φ \varphi in negation normal form (nnf) and a ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}] -interpretation π \pi , we now demonstrate how to extract π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν \pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} from the subgraph of the provenance graph generated based on π \pi over Tl φ → Q ​ ( φ ) \textsc{Tl}_{\varphi\to Q}(\varphi) rooted at the tuple node Q φ ​ ( ν ​ ( free ​ ( φ ) ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\nu(\textsc{free}(\varphi))) .
First, we apply Tl φ → Q ​ ( φ ) \textsc{Tl}_{\varphi\to Q}(\varphi) to compute Q φ Q_{\varphi} .
Then, we generate an instance I π I_{\pi} where the existence of a tuple corresponding to a literal 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) \mathtt{R}({\bf a}) is determined based on the truth value of this literal encoded by its annotation π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) \pi(\mathtt{R}({\bf a})) . A tuple 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) \mathtt{R}({\bf a}) exists in I π I_{\pi} if π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = x \pi(\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=x or π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = 1 \pi(\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=1 and π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = 0 \pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=0 , the tuple is missing if π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = x ¯ \pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=\bar{x} or π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = 1 \pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=1 and π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = 0 \pi(\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=0 , and the tuple’s existence is undetermined if π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = x \pi(\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=x and π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = x ¯ \pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=\bar{x} . Note that this corresponds to the truth value according to the 5 cases we have discerned in Sec.  7.1 .
Next, we generate the provenance graph 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( Q φ , I π ) {\cal PG}(Q_{\varphi},I_{\pi}) . If the formula has free variables, then the provenance graph will contain multiple tuple nodes Q φ ​ ( ν ​ ( free ​ ( φ ) ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\nu(\textsc{free}(\varphi))) , one for each valuation ν \nu of the free variables, and the subgraph rooted at one such tuple node encodes π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν \pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} . By applying a function Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]} (defined in the following), we translate the subgraph rooted at tuple Q φ ​ ( ν ​ ( free ​ ( φ ) ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\nu(\textsc{free}(\varphi))) in 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( Q φ , I π ) {\cal PG}(Q_{\varphi},I_{\pi}) into π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν \pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} .
The function Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]} replaces nodes in the provenance graph with nodes labeled as “ + + ”, “ ⋅ \cdot ”, and annotations of literals. The polynomial π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν \pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} can then be read from the graph generated by Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]} through a top-down traversal. Intuitively, the translation can be explained as follows. Datalog rules are a conjunction of atoms and, thus, are replaced with multiplication. There may exist multiple ways that derive an IDB tuple through the rules of query. That is, IDB tuple nodes represent addition. The exception is IDB tuples that are used in a negated fashion which are replaced with multiplication, because, for the goal to succeed, all derivations of the tuple have to fail. Note that a tuple is used in a negated way if there is an odd number of negated goals on the path between the root of the provenance graph and IDB tuple node. In a program produced by our translation rules, this can only be the case for tuples that correspond to head predicate of a rule computing the ¬ ∃ \neg\,\exists part of the translation of a universal quantification.
Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]} consists of the following steps:
1.
Replace tuple nodes D ​ o ​ m ​ ( 𝐱 ) Dom({\bf x}) with 1 1 .
2.
A goal node connected to an EDB tuple node representing a literal 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) \mathtt{R}({\bf a}) is replaced by π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) \pi(\mathtt{R}({\bf a})) if the goal is positive and π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) \pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}({\bf a})) otherwise.
3.
Next, all EDB tuple nodes are removed leaving the goal nodes formerly connected to EDB tuple nodes to be the new leaves of the graph.
4.
Rule nodes are replaced with multiplication ( ⋅ \cdot ).
5.
Next all remaining goal nodes are replaced with addition ( + + ).
6.
Finally, nodes v t v_{t} corresponding to IDB tuples are replaced with addition with the exception of IDB tuples corresponding to the head predicate ( Q φ ′ Q_{\varphi_{{}^{\prime}}} ) of the second rule of a translated universal quantification which are replaced with multiplication ( ⋅ \cdot ).
Example 8 .
Consider the formula and query from Example 7 . Assume that A = { a , b } A=\{a,b\} and consider that interpretation π \pi which tracks provenance for edge ( a , a ) (a,a) , keeps 𝚁 ⁡ ( b , a ) \mathtt{R}(b,a) undetermined, and sets all other positive literals to false without provenance tracking, i.e., π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( a , a ) ) = x \pi(\mathtt{R}(a,a))\\
=x , π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( a , a ) ) = 0 \pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}(a,a))=0 , π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( b , a ) ) = y \pi(\mathtt{R}(b,a))=y , π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( b , a ) ) = y ¯ \pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}(b,a))=\bar{y} ,
and for all other 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) \mathtt{R}({\bf a}) we have π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = 0 \pi(\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=0 and π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) ) = 1 \pi(\neg\mathtt{R}({\bf a}))=1 . That is, in I π I_{\pi} , tuple 𝚁 ⁡ ( a , a ) \mathtt{R}(a,a) exists, tuple 𝚁 ⁡ ( b , a ) \mathtt{R}(b,a) ’s existence is undetermined, and all other tuples are missing. Fig.  7 shows the provenance graph 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( Q φ , I π ) {\cal PG}(Q_{\varphi},I_{\pi}) . The truth of the universal quantification in φ \varphi is undetermined, because while there exists no a ∈ A a\in A such that ¬ φ 1 \neg\varphi_{1} for ν ​ := ⁡ ( x = a ) \nu\defas(x=a) is true (there is an outgoing edge starting at a a ), the truth of ¬ φ 1 \neg\varphi_{1} is undetermined for ν ​ := ⁡ ( x = b ) \nu\defas(x=b) (the existence of edge 𝚁 ⁡ ( b , a ) \mathtt{R}(b,a) is undetermined). The truth of ∃ y ​ R ​ ( a , y ) \exists y\,R(a,y) and ∃ y ​ 𝚁 ​ ( b , y ) \exists y\,\mathtt{R}(b,y) is justified by the existing tuples 𝚁 ⁡ ( a , a ) \mathtt{R}(a,a) and 𝚁 ⁡ ( b , a ) \mathtt{R}(b,a) , respectively. Applying the translation Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]} , we get graph with node labels shown in Fig.  7 which corresponds to the polynomial 1 ⋅ x ⋅ 1 ⋅ 1 ⋅ y = x ⋅ y = π ⁡ ( φ ) 1\cdot x\cdot 1\cdot 1\cdot y=x\cdot y=\pi(\varphi) .
We are now ready to state the main result of this section: our provenance graphs extended for undetermined facts can encode semiring provenance for first-order (FO) model checking. For simplicity, we only consider sentences, i.e., formulas φ \varphi without free variables, but the result also holds for formulas with free variables by only translating a subgraph of the provenance rooted at the IDB tuple node Q φ ​ ( ν ​ ( free ​ ( φ ) ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\nu(\textsc{free}(\varphi))) which corresponds to the formula ν ⁡ ( φ ) \nu(\varphi) .
Theorem 3 .
Let φ \varphi be a formula, π \pi a ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}] -interpretation, Q ​ := ⁡ Tl φ → Q ​ ( φ ) Q\defas\textsc{Tl}_{\varphi\to Q}(\varphi) , and I π I_{\pi} the instance corresponding to π \pi as defined above. Then
Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] ​ ( 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( Q , I π ) ) = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]}({\cal PG}(Q,I_{\pi}))=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu}
Proof.
We prove the theorem by induction over the structure of the input formula φ \varphi for a given valuation ν \nu of free ​ ( φ ) \textsc{free}(\varphi) . Recall that in I π I_{\pi} the existence of a tuple is determined by the annotations assigned by π \pi to the positive and negated literals corresponding to the tuple. In the following, we will often consider the three cases separately (tuple exists, tuples is missing, tuple’s existence is undetermined), because our the graph produced by our provenance model may differ based on which case applies. Furthermore, it will be beneficial to also prove that tuple Q φ ​ ( ν ​ ( free ​ ( φ ) ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\nu(\textsc{free}(\varphi))) exists/is absent/undetermined iff ν ⁡ ( φ ) \nu(\varphi) is true/false/undetermined, because it allows us to assume that the truth value of a sub-formula corresponds to ν ⁡ ( φ ) \nu(\varphi) . In the following we will use t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} to denote Q φ ​ ( ν ​ ( free ​ ( φ ) ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\nu(\textsc{free}(\varphi))) , i.e., the IDB tuple corresponding to applying the valuation ν \nu to formula φ \varphi . Furthermore, for IDB tuple t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} use g φ , ν g_{{\varphi},{\nu}} to denote the subgraph of the provenance graph rooted at t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} and p φ , ν p_{{\varphi},{\nu}} to denote Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] ​ ( g φ , ν ) \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]}(g_{{\varphi},{\nu}}) .
Base case: φ ​ := ⁡ 𝚁 ​ ( 𝐱 ) \varphi\defas\mathtt{R}({\bf x}) :  
Such formulas are translated into programs with a single rule. The head predicate Q φ Q_{\varphi} for this rule has as arguments the free variables of 𝐱 {\bf x} which for a literal 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \mathtt{R}({\bf x}) are all variables of the literal. For any valuation ν \nu , there is one corresponding tuple Q φ ​ ( ν ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\nu({\bf x})) in the provenance graph which as we have mentioned above we denote as t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} . First consider the case that the tuple t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} exists which is the case when literal 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) \mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})) is true. Applying Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]} to the subgraph corresponding to such a tuple, the tuple node Q φ ​ ( ν ​ ( 𝐱 ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\nu({\bf x})) is mapped to + + , the rule node to ⋅ \cdot , the goal node connected to the EDB node is replaced with π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) ) \pi(\mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x}))) . The resulting expression tree is + ( ⋅ ( π ( 𝚁 ( ν ( 𝐱 ) ) ) ) ) +(\cdot(\pi(\mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x}))))) which evaluates to π ⁡ ( 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) ) = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν \pi(\mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})))=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} . If the existence of 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) \mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})) is undetermined, then the rule deriving the result tuple t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} are also undetermined. However, the resulting expression is still the same. If tuple 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) \mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})) is missing we still get a graph with the same structure.
Base case: ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \neg\,\mathtt{R}({\bf x}) :   A formula
¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \neg\,\mathtt{R}({\bf x}) is translated into a single rule that “joins” the
atom ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \neg\,\mathtt{R}({\bf x}) with multiple D ​ o ​ m Dom atoms (one for each
variable in 𝐱 {\bf x} ). The arguments of the head predicate of the rule are all
variables in 𝐱 {\bf x} . For a given valuation ν \nu , there exists one IDB
tuple node t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} . We consider three cases based on the truth of
literal 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) \mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})) . If the tuple exists then there exists one
successful binding of the rule connected through its goals to | 𝐱 | |{{\bf x}}|
copies of D ​ o ​ m Dom — one for each value in ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \nu({\bf x}) and to a tuple node
𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) \mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})) . Applying the translation and ignoring redundant
additions and multiplications (nodes with a single child) we get an expression
1 ⋅ … ⋅ 1 ⏟ | 𝐱 | ⋅ π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) ) = π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν CLOSE \underbrace{1\cdot\ldots\cdot 1}_{|{{\bf x}}|}\cdot\pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})))=\pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x}))=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} . If 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) \mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})) is undetermined, then the
existence of tuple t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} and the status of the rule deriving it
are also undetermined. Thus, the structure of the graph is the same as in the
first case and the translation returns the same dual provenance polynomial and
we have π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) ) = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν \pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})))=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} . Finally,
if literal 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \mathtt{R}({\bf x}) is false then tuple t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} is
absent from the query result and the derivation producing it failed. Since
failed derivations are only connected to failed goals, the expression created by
Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]} is
⋅ ( π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) ) ) = π ⁡ ( ¬ 𝚁 ⁡ ( ν ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) ) = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν \cdot(\pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x}))))=\pi(\neg\,\mathtt{R}(\nu({\bf x})))=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} .
Inductive step:  
We assume that the statement holds for formulas φ 1 \varphi_{1} and/or φ 2 \varphi_{2} and show that it also holds for a formula φ \varphi .
φ ​ := ⁡ φ 1 ∧ φ 2 \varphi\defas\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2} :  
We have π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν = π ​ ⟦ φ 1 ⟧ ν ⋅ π ​ ⟦ φ 2 ⟧ ν \pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu}=\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{1}}\rrbracket_{\nu}\cdot\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{2}}\rrbracket_{\nu} for valuation ν \nu . The program Tl φ → Q ​ ( φ ) \textsc{Tl}_{\varphi\to Q}(\varphi) consists of single rule Q φ ​ ( free ​ ( φ ) ) : − Q φ 1 ​ ( free ​ ( φ 1 ) ) , Q φ 1 ​ ( free ​ ( φ 2 ) ) Q_{\varphi}(\textsc{free}(\varphi))\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,Q_{\varphi_{1}}(\textsc{free}(\varphi_{1})),Q_{\varphi_{1}}(\textsc{free}(\varphi_{2})) . For a valuation ν \nu , t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} exists if φ 1 ∧ φ 2 \varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2} evaluates to true. In this case, t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} is connected through a rule and two goal nodes to the subgraphs g φ 1 , ν g_{{\varphi_{1}},{\nu}} and g φ 2 , ν g_{{\varphi_{2}},{\nu}} .
Let p i = p φ i , ν p_{i}=p_{{\varphi_{i}},{\nu}} for i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} and p = p φ , ν p=p_{{\varphi},{\nu}} .

Node t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} is undetermined, if one input is undetermined and the other is either undetermined or true. In this case we get the same provenance expression. Finally, t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} is missing if at least one of the inputs is missing. In this case the provenance graph only contains a subgraph for missing or undetermined inputs. That is, there are three possible provenance expressions extracted from the graph p 1 = π ​ ⟦ φ 1 ⟧ ν p_{1}=\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{1}}\rrbracket_{\nu} , p 2 = π ​ ⟦ φ 2 ⟧ ν p_{2}=\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{2}}\rrbracket_{\nu} , or p 1 ⋅ p 2 = π ​ ⟦ φ 1 ⟧ ν ⋅ π ​ ⟦ φ 2 ⟧ ν p_{1}\cdot p_{2}=\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{1}}\rrbracket_{\nu}\cdot\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{2}}\rrbracket_{\nu} . We claim that in all three cases p = 0 = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν p=0=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} . Consider a failed input π ​ ⟦ φ i ⟧ ν \pi\llbracket{\varphi_{i}}\rrbracket_{\nu} for i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} . If φ i \varphi_{i} evaluates to false, then π ​ ⟦ φ i ⟧ ν = 0 \pi\llbracket{\varphi_{i}}\rrbracket_{\nu}=0 . Then p i = 0 p_{i}=0 , the subgraph g i g_{i} is connected to node t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} , and it follows that p = 0 p=0 .
φ ​ := ⁡ φ 1 ∨ φ 2 \varphi\defas\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2} :   We have
π ​ ⟦ φ 1 ∨ φ 2 ⟧ ν = π ​ ⟦ φ 1 ⟧ ν + π ​ ⟦ φ 2 ⟧ ν \pi\llbracket{\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}}\rrbracket_{\nu}=\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{1}}\rrbracket_{\nu}+\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{2}}\rrbracket_{\nu} .
A disjunction is translated into two rules (a union) corresponding to joining
Q φ i Q_{\varphi_{i}} for i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} with a number of copies of D ​ o ​ m Dom such that
both rules return free ​ ( φ ) \textsc{free}(\varphi) . Tuple t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} exists as long
as one tuple t φ i , ν t_{{\varphi_{i}},{\nu}} for i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} exists. In this case the
tuple will be connected through successful or undetermined derivations to nodes
t φ i , ν t_{{\varphi_{i}},{\nu}} . Let p = p φ , ν p=p_{{\varphi},{\nu}}
and p i = p φ i , ν p_{i}=p_{{\varphi_{i}},{\nu}} . We have either p = p 1 p=p_{1} ,

p = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν p=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} . Now consider the case where p = p 1 p=p_{1} (the case for

Formula φ 1 ∨ φ 2 \varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2} evaluates to false or

the provenance graph will contain both g 1 g_{1} and g 2 g_{2} and

φ ​ := ​ ∃ x ​ φ 1 \varphi\defas\exists x\,\varphi_{1} :  
We have π ⟦ φ ⟧ ν = ∑ a ∈ A π ⟦ φ 1 ⟧ ν [ x ↦ a ] \pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu}=\sum_{a\in A}\pi\llbracket{\varphi_{1}}\rrbracket_{\nu[x\mapsto a]} .
There is one rule deriving Q φ Q_{\varphi} which binds x x , the variable bound by the existential quantifier, to D ​ o ​ m Dom (all values from A A ) and φ 1 \varphi_{1} . Tuple t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} exists iff there exists at least one a ∈ A a\in A for which ν [ x ↦ a ] ( φ 1 ) \nu[x\mapsto a](\varphi_{1}) is true which based on the induction hypothesis implies that tuple t φ 1 , ν [ x ↦ a ] t_{{\varphi_{1}},{\nu[x\mapsto a]}}
exists. Recall that an existing tuple is connected to successful and undetermined rule derivations.
Let p a = p φ 1 , ν [ x ↦ a ] p_{a}=p_{{\varphi_{1}},{\nu[x\mapsto a]}} .
Since the tuple node t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} will be replaced with + + by Tr Expl → ℕ ⁡ [ X , X ¯ ] \textsc{Tr}_{\textsc{Expl}\to\mathbb{N}[X,\bar{X}]} , the expression p p is a sum p = ∑ a ∈ A ∧ ν [ x ↦ a ] ( free ( φ 1 ) ) p a p=\sum_{a\in A\wedge\nu[x\mapsto a](\textsc{free}(\varphi_{1}))}p_{a} . Thus, p = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν p=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} . Tuple t φ , ν t_{{\varphi},{\nu}} is missing if all its derivations fail and undetermined if no derivation is successful and at least one if undetermined. In both cases the provenance graph connects this tuple to all possible derivations and, thus, p = π ​ ⟦ φ ⟧ ν p=\pi\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\nu} .

quantification in Datalog as double negation
( ¬ ∃ x ​ ¬ φ 1 \neg\,\exists x\,\neg\,\varphi_{1} ). Note that there is a single rule

φ ​ := ⁡ x ​ 𝐨𝐩 ⁡ y \varphi\defas x\operatorname{{\bf op}}y :  
The translation creates a single rule consisting of the comparison and atoms D ​ o ​ m ​ ( x ) Dom(x) and D ​ o ​ m ​ ( y ) Dom(y) which ensure safety. ∎
8 Computing Explanations
We now present our approach for computing explanations using Datalog. Our approach generates a Datalog program 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}} by rewriting a given query (input program) P P
to return the edge relation of the explanation
Expl ​ ( P , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}(P,\psi,I)
for a provenance question ( PQ ) ψ \psi .
Recall that a PQ is a pattern describing existing/missing outputs of interest and that an explanation for a PQ is a subgraph of the provenance which contains the provenance of all tuples described by the pattern.
Our approach for computing 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}} consists of the following steps that we describe in detail in the following subsections: 1) we unify the input program P P with the PQ ψ \psi by propagating constants from ψ \psi top-down to prune derivations of outputs that do not match the PQ ; 2) we determine for each IDB predicate whether the explanation may contain existing, missing, or both types of tuples from this predicate. Similarly, for each rule we determine whether successful, failed, all, or no derivations of this rule may occur in the provenance graph; 3) based on restricted and annotated version of the input program produced by the first two steps, we then generate firing rules which capture the variable bindings of successful and failed derivations of the input program’s rules;
4) The result of the firing rules is a superset of the set of relevant provenance fragments. We introduce additional rules that enforce connectivity to remove spurious fragments;
5) finally, we create rules that generate the edge relation of the explanation. This is the only step that depends on what provenance type (e.g., Fig.  4 ) is requested.
In the following, we will illustrate our approach using the
provenance question ψ n , s = Why ​ 𝚀 ​ ( n , s ) \psi_{n,s}=\textsc{Why}\,\mathtt{Q}(n,s) from Example  1 , i.e.,
why New York is connected to Seattle via train with one intermediate stop, but there is no direct connection.
8.1 Unifying the Program with the PQ
The node 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) in the provenance graph (Fig.  2 )
is only connected to derivations which return
𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) . For instance, if variable X X is bound to another city x x (e.g., Chicago) in a
derivation of the rule r 1 r_{1} , then this rule cannot return the tuple ( n , s ) (n,s) .
This reasoning can be applied recursively to replace variables in rules with
constants. That is, we unify the rules in the program top-down with the PQ . This process corresponds to selection push-down for relational algebra expressions. We may create multiple partially
unified versions of a rule or predicate.
For example, to explore successful derivations of 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) , we are interested in both train connections from New York to some city ( 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , Z ) \mathtt{T}(n,Z) ) and from any city to Seattle ( 𝚃 ⁡ ( Z , s ) \mathtt{T}(Z,s) ). Furthermore, we need to know whether there is a direct connection from New York to Seattle ( 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{T}(n,s) ).
We store variable bindings as superscripts to distinguish multiple copies of a rule generated based on different bindings.
Example 9 .
Given the question ψ n , s \psi_{n,s} , we unify the single rule
r 1 r_{1} using the assignment ( X = n , Y = s ) (X{=}n,Y{=}s) :
r 1 ( X = n , Y = s ) : 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) : − 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , Z ) , 𝚃 ⁡ ( Z , s ) , ¬ 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , s ) r_{1}^{(X=n,Y=s)}:\mathtt{Q}(n,s)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{T}(n,Z),\mathtt{T}(Z,s),\neg\,\mathtt{T}(n,s)
This approach is correct because if we bind a variable in the head of rule, then only rule derivations that agree with this binding can derive tuples that agree with this binding.
Based on this unification step, we know which bindings may produce fragments of 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) that are relevant
for explaining the PQ (the pseudocode for the algorithm is presented
in Algorithm  1 ).
For an input P P , we use P U ​ n ​ i ​ f ​ i ​ e ​ d P_{Unified} to denote the result of this unification.

Algorithm 1 Unify Program With PQ
8.2 Add Annotations based on Success/Failure
For Why ​ Q ​ ( t ) \textsc{Why}\,Q(t) ( Whynot ​ Q ​ ( t ) \textsc{Whynot}\,Q(t) ), we are only interested in subgraphs of the provenance rooted at existing (missing) tuple nodes for 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} .
With this information, we can infer restrictions for the success/failure state of nodes in
the provenance graph
that are directly or indirectly connected to PQ node(s) (belong to the explanation).
We store these restrictions as annotations T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} , F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} , and F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}
on heads and goals of rules and use these annotations to guide the generation of rules that capture derivations in step 3.
Here, T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} ( F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} ) indicates that we are only interested in successful (failed) nodes, and F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} that we are interested in both.
Example 10 .
Continuing with our running example question ψ n , s \psi_{n,s} , we know that 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) is in the result (Fig.  1 ). This implies that only successful rule nodes and their successful goal nodes can be connected to this tuple node.
Note that this annotation
only indicates that it is sufficient to focus on successful rule derivations
since failed ones cannot be connected to 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) .
r 1 ( X = n , Y = s ) , T : 𝚀 ​ ( n , s ) T : − 𝚃 ​ ( n , Z ) T , 𝚃 ​ ( Z , s ) T , ¬ 𝚃 ​ ( n , s ) T \displaystyle r_{1}^{(X=n,Y=s),{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}:\mathtt{Q}(n,s)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{T}(n,Z)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}},\mathtt{T}(Z,s)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}},\neg\,\mathtt{T}(n,s)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}
We now propagate the annotations of the goals in r 1 r_{1} throughout the program.
That is, for any goal that is an IDB predicate, we propagate its annotation to the head of all rules deriving the goal’s predicate
and, then, propagate these annotations to the corresponding rule bodies.
Note that the inverted annotation is propagated for negated goals (e.g., ¬ 𝚃 ​ ( n , s ) T \neg\,\mathtt{T}(n,s)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} ). For instance, if 𝚃 \mathtt{T} would be an IDB predicate, then we would annotate the head of all rules deriving 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{T}(n,s) with F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} , because 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) can only exist if 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{T}(n,s) does not exist.
Partially unified atoms such as 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , Z ) \mathtt{T}(n,Z) may occur in both negative and positive goals.
We annotate such atoms with F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} . The algorithm generating the annotation consists of the steps shown below (the pseudocode is presented
in Algorithm  2 ).
We use P A ​ n ​ n ​ o ​ t P_{Annot} to denote the result of this algorithm for P U ​ n ​ i ​ f ​ i ​ e ​ d P_{Unified} (input to this step).
1.
Annotate the head of all rules deriving tuples matching
the question with T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} (why) or F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} (why-not).
2.
Repeat the following steps until a fixpoint is reached:
(a)
Propagate the annotation of a rule head to goals in the rule body as follows: propagate T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} for T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} annotated heads and F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} for F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} annotated heads.
(b)
For each annotated positive goal in the rule body, we propagate its annotation ( F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} , T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} , or F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} ) to all rules that have this atom in the head. For negated goals, we propagate the inverted annotation (e.g., F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} for T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} ) unless the annotation is F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} in which case we propagate F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} .

Algorithm 2 Success/Failure Annotations
8.3 Creating Firing Rules
To compute the relevant subgraph of 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) (the explanation) for a PQ , we need to determine successful and/or failed rule derivations.
Each derivation paired with the information whether it is successful
over the given database (and which goals are failed in case it is not
successful) is sufficient for generating a fragment of 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) .
Successful derivations are always part of 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) for a given query (input program) P P whereas failed
rule derivations only appear
if the tuple in the head failed,
i.e., there are no successful derivations of any rule with
this head. To capture the variable bindings of successful/failed rule derivations, we create
“firing rules” .
For successful rule derivations, a firing rule consists of the body of the rule
(but using the firing version of each predicate in the body) and
a new head predicate that contains all variables used in the rule.
In this way, the firing rule captures all the variable bindings of a rule derivation.
The rationale behind this is that rule derivations apply a valuation to assign constants to the variables of a rule. A rule derivation is successful if after applying the valuation to the body, all goals are successful. For any successful rule derivation, the result of applying the valuation to the head is part of the result of the program containing the rule. Thus, if for a rule r r we create a firing rule that has the same body as r r , but has all body variables in the head, then the IDB predicate computed by the firing rule stores all successful derivations of r r .
Furthermore, for each IDB predicate R R that occurs as a head of a rule r r , we create a firing rule that has the firing version of predicate R R in the head and firing version of the rules r r deriving the predicate in the body.
These rules capture existing IDB tuples.
For EDB predicates, we create firing rules that have the firing version of the predicate in the head and the EDB predicate in the body.
These rules create copies of EDB relations. Note that for the positive case these rules are not strictly necessary, but as we will show they are necessary once negation and missing answers are introduced.
 
𝙵 𝚀 , T ​ ( n , s ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s)
: − 𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , T ​ ( n , s , Z ) \displaystyle\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s,Z)
𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , T ​ ( n , s , Z ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s,Z)
: − 𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( n , Z ) , 𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( Z , s ) , 𝙵 𝚃 , F ​ ( n , s ) \displaystyle\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,Z),\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(Z,s),\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(n,s)
𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( n , Z ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,Z)
: − 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , Z ) \displaystyle\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{T}(n,Z)
𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( Z , s ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(Z,s)
: − 𝚃 ⁡ ( Z , s ) \displaystyle\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{T}(Z,s)
𝙵 𝚃 , F ​ ( n , s ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(n,s)
: − ¬ 𝚃 ( n , s ) \displaystyle\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\neg\,\mathtt{T}(n,s)
Figure 8: Example firing rules for Why ​ 𝚀 ​ ( n , s ) \textsc{Why}\,\mathtt{Q}(n,s)
Example 11 .
Consider the annotated program in Example  10 for the question ψ n , s = Why ​ 𝚀 ​ ( n , s ) \psi_{n,s}=\textsc{Why}\,\mathtt{Q}(n,s) .
We generate the firing rules shown in Fig.  8 .
The firing rule for r 1 ( X = n , Y = s ) , T r_{1}^{(X=n,Y=s),{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} (the second rule from the top) is derived
from the rule r 1 r_{1} by adding Z Z
(the only existential variable) to the head,
renaming the head predicate as 𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} ,
and replacing each goal with its firing version
(e.g., 𝙵 𝚃 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} for the two positive goals and 𝙵 𝚃 , F \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} for the negated goal).
Note that negated goals are replaced with firing rules that have inverted annotations
(e.g., the goal ¬ 𝚃 ​ ( n , s ) T \neg\,\mathtt{T}(n,s)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} is replaced with 𝙵 𝚃 , F ​ ( n , s ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(n,s) ).
In the following, we will define rules with annotation F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} to capture missing tuples of an EDB or IDB predicate and failed rule derivations.
Furthermore, we introduce firing rules for EDB tuples (three rules at the bottom).
Evaluated over the example instance from Fig.  1 these rules produce the following instance:
𝙵 𝚀 , T ​ ( n , s ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s)
𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , T ​ ( n , s , c ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s,c)
𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , T ​ ( n , s , w ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s,w)
𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( n , c ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,c)
𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( n , w ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,w)
𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( c , s ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(c,s)
𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( s , s ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(s,s)
𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( w , s ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(w,s)
𝙵 𝚃 , F ​ ( n , s ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(n,s)
We, now, extend firing rules to support queries with negation and capture missing answers.
To construct a 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) fragment corresponding to a missing tuple,
we need to find failed rule derivations with the tuple in the head
and ensure that no successful derivations with this head exist (otherwise, we may capture irrelevant failed derivations of existing tuples).
In addition, we need to determine which goals are failed for a failed rule derivation because only failed goals are connected to the node representing the failed rule derivation in the
provenance graph.
To capture this information, we add additional boolean variables — V i V_{i} for goal g i g^{i} — to the head of a firing rule that record for each goal whether it failed or not.
The body of a firing rule for failed rule derivations is created by
replacing every goal in the body with its F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} firing version, and
adding the firing version of the negated head to the body (to ensure that only bindings for missing tuples are captured). Firing rules capturing failed derivations use the F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} firing versions of their goals because not all goals of a failed derivation have to be failed and the failure status determines whether the corresponding goal node is part of the explanation.
A F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} firing rule for a predicate 𝚁 \mathtt{R} captures all tuples in Tup ​ ( 𝚁 ) \textsc{Tup}(\mathtt{R}) no matter whether they exist or not. An additional boolean attribute is used to store for each such tuple whether it exists or not.
Example 12 .
Consider an EDB relation 𝚁 ⁡ ( 𝙰 , 𝙱 ) \mathtt{R(A,B)} , domain assignment 𝑑𝑜𝑚 ( 𝚁 . 𝙰 ) = 𝑑𝑜𝑚 ( 𝚁 . 𝙱 ) = { a , b } \mathit{dom}(\mathtt{R.A})=\mathit{dom}(\mathtt{R.B})=\{a,b\} and instance { 𝚁 ⁡ ( a , a ) , 𝚁 ⁡ ( b , b ) } \{\mathtt{R}(a,a),\mathtt{R}(b,b)\} . The firing rules for 𝙵 𝚁 , F / T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} using the queries 𝑑𝑜𝑚 𝚁 . 𝙰 \mathit{dom}_{\mathtt{R.A}} and 𝑑𝑜𝑚 𝚁 . 𝙱 \mathit{dom}_{\mathtt{R.B}} provided by the user to compute the domain assignment are:
𝙵 𝚁 , F / T ​ ( X , Y , t ​ r ​ u ​ e ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(X,Y,true)
: − 𝙵 𝚁 , T ​ ( X , Y ) \displaystyle\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(X,Y)
𝙵 𝚁 , F / T ​ ( X , Y , f ​ a ​ l ​ s ​ e ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(X,Y,false)

The third rule computes existing tuples creating a copy of relation 𝚁 \mathtt{R} . The fourth rule, enumerates all tuples in Tup ​ ( 𝚁 ) \textsc{Tup}(\mathtt{R}) using the domain assignment and only returns tuples that do not exist. The first and the second rule then combine the results of the last two rules and store whether a tuple exists as a boolean value in an additional attribute.
Evaluating these rules we generate the following instance:
𝙵 𝚁 , F / T ​ ( a , a , t ​ r ​ u ​ e ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(a,a,true)
𝙵 𝚁 , F / T ​ ( b , b , t ​ r ​ u ​ e ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(b,b,true)
𝙵 𝚁 , F / T ​ ( a , b , f ​ a ​ l ​ s ​ e ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(a,b,false)
𝙵 𝚁 , F / T ​ ( b , a , f ​ a ​ l ​ s ​ e ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(b,a,false)
A firing rule capturing missing tuples may not be safe, i.e., it may contain variables that only occur in negated goals. These variables should be restricted to the associated domains for the attributes
the variables are bound to. Recall that associated domain 𝑑𝑜𝑚 ( 𝚁 . 𝙰 ) \mathit{dom}(\mathtt{R.A}) for an attribute 𝚁 . 𝙰 \mathtt{R.A} is given as an unary query 𝑑𝑜𝑚 𝚁 . 𝙰 \mathit{dom}_{\mathtt{R.A}} . We use these queries in firing rules to restrict the values
a variable is bound to. Thus, we ensure that only missing answers formed from the associated domains are considered and that firing rules are safe.
 
𝙵 𝚀 , F ​ ( s , n ) \displaystyle\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(s,n)

Figure 9: Example firing rules for Whynot ​ 𝚀 ​ ( s , n ) \textsc{Whynot}\,\mathtt{Q}(s,n)
Example 13 .
Consider the question Whynot ​ 𝚀 ​ ( s , n ) \textsc{Whynot}\,\mathtt{Q}(s,n) from Example  1 .
The firing rules generated for this question are in Fig.  9 .
We exclude the rules for the second goal
𝚃 ⁡ ( Z , n ) \mathtt{T}(Z,n) and the negated goal ¬ 𝚃 ⁡ ( s , n ) \neg\,\mathtt{T}(s,n) which are analogous to the rules
for the first goal 𝚃 ⁡ ( s , Z ) \mathtt{T}(s,Z) .
New York cannot be reached from Seattle with exactly one transfer, i.e., 𝚀 ⁡ ( s , n ) \mathtt{Q}(s,n) is not in the result. Thus, we are only interested in failed derivations of rule r 1 r_{1} with X = s X{=}s and Y = n Y{=}n .
Furthermore, each rule node in the provenance graph corresponding to such a derivation
will only be connected to failed subgoals.
Thus, we need to capture which goals are successful or failed
for each such failed derivation.
We model this using boolean variables V 1 V_{1} , V 2 V_{2} , and V 3 V_{3}
(one for each goal) that are set to true iff the tuple corresponding to the goal exists.
The firing version 𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , F ​ ( s , n , Z , V 1 , V 2 , ¬ V 3 ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(s,n,Z,V_{1},V_{2},\neg\,V_{3}) of r 1 r_{1} returns
all variable bindings for derivations of r 1 r_{1} such that 𝚀 ⁡ ( s , n ) \mathtt{Q}(s,n) is the head
(i.e., guaranteed by adding 𝙵 𝚀 , F ​ ( s , n ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(s,n) to the body),
the rule derivations are failed, and the tuple corresponding to the i t ​ h i^{th} goal exists for this binding iff V i V_{i} is true. The failure status of the i t ​ h i^{th} goal is V i V_{i} for positive goals and ¬ V i \neg V_{i} for negated goals. To produce all these bindings, we need rules capturing successful and failed tuple nodes
for each subgoal of the rule r 1 r_{1} .
We annotate such rules with F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} and use a boolean variable (true or false) to record
whether a tuple exists
(e.g., 𝙵 𝚃 , F / T ​ ( s , Z , t ​ r ​ u ​ e ) : − 𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( s , Z ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(s,Z,true)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(s,Z) is one of these rules).
Similarly, 𝙵 𝚃 , F / T ​ ( s , n , f ​ a ​ l ​ s ​ e ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(s,n,false)
represents the fact that tuple 𝚃 ⁡ ( s , n ) \mathtt{T}(s,n) (connection from Seattle to New York)
is missing. This causes the third goal of r 1 r_{1} to succeed for any derivation where X = s X{=}s and Y = n Y{=}n .
For each unified EDB atom annotated with F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} , we create four rules: one for existing tuples
(e.g., 𝙵 𝚃 , T ​ ( s , Z ) : − 𝚃 ⁡ ( s , Z ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(s,Z)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{T}(s,Z) ),
one for the failure case (e.g., 𝙵 𝚃 , F ​ ( s , Z ) : − 𝑑𝑜𝑚 𝚃 . 𝚝𝚘𝙲𝚒𝚝𝚢 ​ ( Z ) , ¬ 𝚃 ⁡ ( s , Z ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{T},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(s,Z)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathit{dom}_{\mathtt{T.toCity}}(Z),\neg\,\mathtt{T}(s,Z) ), and two for the F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} version.
For the failure case,
we use predicate 𝑑𝑜𝑚 𝚃 . 𝚝𝚘𝙲𝚒𝚝𝚢 \mathit{dom}_{\mathtt{T.toCity}} to only consider missing tuples ( s , Z ) (s,Z) where Z Z is a value from the associated domain.
Algorithm  3 takes as input the program P A ​ n ​ n ​ o ​ t P_{Annot} produced by step 2 and outputs a program P F ​ i ​ r ​ e {P}_{Fire} containing firing rules. The pseudocode for the subprocedures is presented
in Algorithm  4 . The algorithm maintains a queue t ​ o ​ d ​ o todo of annotated atoms that have to be processed which is initialized with Pattern ​ ( ψ ) \textsc{Pattern}(\psi) , i.e., the provenance question atom. Furthermore, we maintain a set d ​ o ​ n ​ e done of atoms that have been processed already. Variables t ​ o ​ d ​ o todo , d ​ o ​ n ​ e done , and P F ​ i ​ r ​ e {P}_{Fire} are global variables that are shared with the subprocedures of this algorithm. For each atom
R ​ ( t ) σ R(t)^{\sigma} (line 8) from the queue (here σ \sigma is the annotation of the atom, e.g., F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} ),
we mark the atom as done (line 9). We then consider two cases: R R is an EDB atom or an IDB atom in which case we have to create firing rules for the predicate (relation) and the rules deriving it.
Recall that an EDB atom is a relation in the schema over the input Datalog program P P and
an IDB atom is the head atom of the rule(s) in P P .
The firing rules for EDB predicates check whether the tuples do or do not exist.
These rules allow us to determine the success or failure of goals corresponding EDB predicates in rule derivations. For IDB predicates, we create firing rules that determine their existance based on successful or failed rule derivations captured by firing rules for the rules of the program. Consider a given program P P with two rules:
1) r 1 : 𝚀 ⁡ ( X ) : − 𝚁 ⁡ ( X , Y ) , 𝚀 𝟷 ​ ( Y ) r_{1}:\mathtt{Q}(X)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{R}(X,Y),\mathtt{Q_{1}}(Y) and 2) r 2 : 𝚀 𝟷 ​ ( Y ) : − 𝚂 ⁡ ( Y , Z ) r_{2}:\mathtt{Q_{1}}(Y)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{S}(Y,Z)
where 𝚁 \mathtt{R} and 𝚂 \mathtt{S} are EDB relations and 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} and 𝚀 𝟷 \mathtt{Q_{1}} are IDB predicates.
To capture provenance for the predicate 𝚀 ⁡ ( X ) \mathtt{Q}(X) ,
we create firing rules for 𝚁 \mathtt{R} and 𝚂 \mathtt{S} to check existence or absence of tuples matching t t in 𝚁 \mathtt{R} and 𝚂 \mathtt{S} .
Moreover, we also generate firing rules for rules r 1 r_{1} and r 2 r_{2}
to explain how derivations of 𝚀 ⁡ ( X ) \mathtt{Q}(X) through these rules have succeeded or failed. The firing rule for r 1 r_{1} uses the firing rule for IDB predicate 𝚀 𝟷 \mathtt{Q_{1}}
which in turn uses the firing rule for r 2 r_{2} since h ​ e ​ a ​ d ​ ( r 2 ) = 𝚀 𝟷 head(r_{2})=\mathtt{Q_{1}} .
We describe these two cases in the following.
EDB atoms (line 13).
For an EDB atom R ​ ( t ) T R(t)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} , we use procedure createEDBFiringRule to create one rule 𝙵 𝚁 , T ​ ( t ) : − R ​ ( t ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(t)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,R(t) that returns tuples from relation R R that match t t . For missing tuples ( R ​ ( t ) F R(t)^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} ), we extract all variables from t t (some arguments may be constants propagated during unification) and create a rule that returns all tuples that can be formed from values of the associated domains of the attributes these variables are bound to and do not exist in R R . This is achieved by adding goals 𝑑𝑜𝑚 ⁡ ( X i ) \mathit{dom}{(X_{i})}
as explained in Example  13 .

Algorithm 3 Create Firing Rules
IDB atoms (lines 13-19).
IDB atoms with F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} or F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} annotations are handled in the same way as EDB atoms with these annotations.
If the atom is R ​ ( t ) F R(t)^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} (line 13), we create a rule with ¬ 𝙵 𝚁 , T ​ ( t ) \neg\,\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(t) in the body using the associated domain queries to restrict variable bindings. Similarly, for R ​ ( t ) F / T R(t)^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} , the procedure called in line 13 adds two additional rules as shown in Fig.  9 ( 5 t ​ h 5^{th} and 6 t ​ h 6^{th} rule) for EDB atoms.
Both types of rules only use the positive firing version for R ⁡ ( t ) R(t) and domain predicates in their body. Thus, these rules are independent of which rules derive R R .
Now, for any R R , we create positive firing rules that correspond to the derivation of R R through one particular rule. For that, we iterate over the annotated versions of all rules deriving R R (lines 14+15).
For each rule r r with head 𝚁 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{R}(t) , we create a rule 𝙵 𝚁 , T ​ ( t ) : − 𝙵 𝚛 , T ​ ( X → ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(t)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(\vec{X}) where X → \vec{X} is the concatenation of t t with all existential variables from the body of r r .
Rules (line 15-19).
Consider a rule r : R ⁡ ( t ) : − g 1 ​ ( X 1 → ) , … , g n ​ ( X n → ) r:R(t)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,g_{1}(\vec{X_{1}}),\\
\ldots,g_{n}(\vec{X_{n}}) .
If the head of r r is annotated with T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} , then we create a rule with head 𝙵 𝚛 , T ​ ( X → ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(\vec{X}) where X → = v ​ a ​ r ​ s ​ ( r ) \vec{X}=vars(r) (stored in variable a ​ r ​ g ​ s args , lines 16+17) and the same body as r r except that each goal is replaced with its firing version with appropriate annotation (e.g., T {\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} for positive goals). For rules annotated with F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} or F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} ,
we create one additional rule with head 𝙵 𝚛 , F ​ ( X → , V → ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(\vec{X},\vec{V}) where X → \vec{X} is defined as above, and V → \vec{V} contains V i V_{i} if the i t ​ h i^{th} goal of r r is positive and ¬ V i \neg\,V_{i} otherwise. The body of this rule contains the F / T {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T} version of every goal in r r ’s body plus an additional goal 𝙵 𝚁 , F \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} to ensure that the head atom is failed. As an example for this type of rule, consider the third rule from the top in Fig.  9 .

Algorithm 4 Create Firing Rules Subprocedures
Theorem 4 (Correctness of Firing Rules) .
Let P P be an input program, r r denote a rule of P P with m m goals, and P F ​ i ​ r ​ e {P}_{Fire} be the firing version of P P . We use r ⁡ ( t ) ⊧ P ⁡ ( I ) r(t)\models P(I) to denote that the rule derivation r ⁡ ( t ) r(t) is successful in the evaluation of program P P over I I . The firing rules for P P correctly determine existence of tuples, successful derivations, and failed derivations for missing answers:
•
𝙵 𝚁 , T ​ ( t ) ∈ P F ​ i ​ r ​ e ​ ( I ) ↔ R ⁡ ( t ) ∈ P ⁡ ( I ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(t)\in{P}_{Fire}(I)\leftrightarrow R(t)\in P(I)
•
𝙵 𝚁 , F ​ ( t ) ∈ P F ​ i ​ r ​ e ​ ( I ) ↔ R ⁡ ( t ) ∉ P ⁡ ( I ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(t)\in{P}_{Fire}(I)\leftrightarrow R(t)\not\in P(I)
•
𝙵 𝚛 , T ​ ( t ) ∈ P F ​ i ​ r ​ e ​ ( I ) ↔ r ⁡ ( t ) ⊧ P ⁡ ( I ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(t)\in{P}_{Fire}(I)\leftrightarrow r(t)\models P(I)
•
𝙵 𝚛 , F ​ ( t , V → ) ∈ P F ​ i ​ r ​ e ​ ( I ) ↔ r ⁡ ( t ) ⊧̸ P ⁡ ( I ) ∧ h ​ e ​ a ​ d ​ ( r ⁡ ( t ) ) ∉ P ⁡ ( I ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(t,\vec{V})\in{P}_{Fire}(I)\leftrightarrow r(t)\not\models P(I)\wedge head(r(t))\not\in P(I) and for i ∈ { 1 , … , m } i\in\{1,\ldots,m\} we have that V i V_{i} is false iff i t ​ h i^{th} goal fails in r ⁡ ( t ) r(t) .
Proof.
We prove Theorem  4 by induction over the “depth” of a program.
We define the depth d d of predicates, rules, and programs as follows: 1) for all EDB predicates R R , we define d ⁡ ( R ) = 0 d(R)=0 ; 2) for an IDB predicate R R , we define d ⁡ ( R ) = max h ​ e ​ a ​ d ​ ( r ) = R ⁡ d ⁡ ( r ) d(R)=\max_{head(r)=R}d(r) , i.e., the maximal depth among all rules r r with h ​ e ​ a ​ d ​ ( r ) = R head(r)=R ; 3) the depth of a rule r r is d ⁡ ( r ) = max R ∈ b ​ o ​ d ​ y ​ ( r ) ⁡ d ⁡ ( R ) + 1 d(r)=\max_{R\in body(r)}d(R)+1 , i.e., the maximal depth of all predicates in its body plus one; 4) the depth of a program P P is the maximum depth of its rules: d ⁡ ( P ) = max r ∈ P ⁡ d ⁡ ( r ) d(P)=\max_{r\in P}d(r) .
1) Base Case.
Assume that program P P has depth 1 1 ,
e.g., r : 𝚀 ⁡ ( X → ) : − 𝚁 ⁡ ( X 1 → ) , … , 𝚁 ⁡ ( X n → ) r:\mathtt{Q}(\vec{X})\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{R}(\vec{X_{1}}),\ldots,\mathtt{R}(\vec{X_{n}}) . We first prove that firing rules for EDB atoms are correct,
because only these rules are used for the rules of depth 1 1 programs.
A positive version of EDB firing rule 𝙵 𝚁 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} creates a copy of the input relation 𝚁 \mathtt{R} and, thus, ∀ t : t ∈ R ⇔ t ∈ 𝙵 𝚁 , T \forall t:t\in R\Leftrightarrow t\in\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} .
For the negative version 𝙵 𝚁 , F \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} , all variables are bound to associated domains 𝑑𝑜𝑚 \mathit{dom}
and it is explicitly checked that ¬ R ⁡ ( X → ) \neg\,R(\vec{X}) holds.
Finally, 𝙵 𝚁 , F / T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} uses 𝙵 𝚁 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} and 𝙵 𝚁 , F \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} to determine whether the tuple exists in 𝚁 \mathtt{R} .
Since these rules are correct, it follows that 𝙵 𝚁 , F / T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} is correct.
The positive firing rule for the rule r r ( 𝙵 𝚛 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} ) is correct since its body only contains positive and negative EDB firing rules ( 𝙵 𝚁 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} and 𝙵 𝚁 , F \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} , respectively) which are already known to be correct.
The correctness of the positive firing version of a rule’s head predicate ( 𝙵 𝚀 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} ) follows naturally from the correctness of 𝙵 𝚛 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} .
The negative version of the rule 𝙵 𝚛 , F ​ ( X → , V → ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(\vec{X},\vec{V}) contains an additional goal (i.e., ¬ 𝚀 ⁡ ( X → ) \neg\,\mathtt{Q}(\vec{X}) )
and uses the firing version 𝙵 𝚁 , F / T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{R},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}/{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} to return only bindings for failed derivations.
For a head predicate with annotation F {\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F} , we create two firing rules ( 𝙵 𝚀 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} and 𝙵 𝚀 , F \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} ).
The rule 𝙵 𝚀 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} was already proven to be correct.
𝙵 𝚀 , F \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}} is also correct, because it contains only 𝙵 𝚀 , T \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}} and domain queries in the body which were already proven to be correct.
2) Inductive Step.
It remains to be shown that firing rules for programs of depth n + 1 n+1 are correct.
Assume that firing rules for programs of depth up to n n are correct.
Let r r be a firing rule of depth n + 1 n+1 in a program of depth n + 1 n+1 .
It follows that max R ∈ b ​ o ​ d ​ y ​ ( r ) ⁡ d ⁡ ( R ) ≤ n \max_{R\in body(r)}d(R)\leq n ,
otherwise r r would be of a depth larger than n + 1 n+1 . Based on the induction hypothesis, it is guaranteed that the firing rules for all these predicates are correct.
Using the same argument as in the base case, it follows that the firing rule for r r is correct.
∎

Figure 10: Example firing rules with connectivity checks
8.4 Connectivity Joins
To be in the result of a firing rule is a necessary,
but not sufficient, condition for the corresponding rule node to be
connected to a node Q ​ ( t ′ ) ∈ Match ​ ( ψ ) Q(t^{\prime})\in\textsc{Match}(\psi) in the explanation. Thus, we have to check connectivity of intermediate results explicitly.
Example 14 .
Consider the firing rules for
ψ n , s \psi_{n,s} shown in Fig.  8 .
The corresponding rules with connectivity checks are shown in Fig.  10 .
All rule nodes corresponding to
𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , T ​ ( n , s , Z ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s,Z) are guaranteed to be connected to the node 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) (corresponding to the only atom in Match ​ ( ψ n , s ) \textsc{Match}(\psi_{n,s}) ).
Note that connectivity joins are also required for negative firing rules
(e.g., 𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , F ​ ( s , n , Z , V 1 , V 2 , ¬ V 3 ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(s,n,Z,V_{1},V_{2},\neg\,V_{3}) in Fig.  9
is used for Whynot  ). For sake of example, assume that instead of using 𝚃 \mathtt{T} , rule r 1 r_{1} uses an IDB relation R R which is computed using a rule r 2 : 𝚁 ⁡ ( X , Y ) : − 𝚃 ⁡ ( X , Y ) r_{2}:\mathtt{R}(X,Y)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{T}(X,Y) .
Consider the firing rule 𝙵 𝚛 𝟸 , T ​ ( n , Z ) : − 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , Z ) \mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,Z)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{T}(n,Z) created based on the 1 s ​ t 1^{st} goal of r 1 r_{1} .
Some provenance fragments computed by this rule may not be connected to 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) .
A tuple node 𝚁 ⁡ ( n , c ) \mathtt{R}(n,c) for a constant c c is only connected to the node 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) iff it is
part of a successful binding of r 1 r_{1} .
That is, for the node 𝚁 ⁡ ( n , c ) \mathtt{R}(n,c) , there has to exist a tuple 𝚁 ⁡ ( c , s ) \mathtt{R}(c,s) . Connectivity is achieved by adding the head of the firing rule for r 1 r_{1} to the body of the firing rule for r 2 r_{2} as shown in Fig.  10
(the 3 r ​ d 3^{rd} and 4 t ​ h 4^{th} rule).
Our algorithm
traverses the query’s rules starting from PQ atom(s) to find all combinations of rules r i r_{i} and r j r_{j} such that the head of r j r_{j} can be unified with a goal in r i r_{i} ’s body.
For each such pair ( r i , r j ) (r_{i},r_{j}) where the head of r j r_{j} corresponds to the k t ​ h k^{th} goal in the body of r i r_{i} , we create a rule
𝙵𝙲 𝚛 𝚓 , 𝚛 𝚒 𝚔 , 𝚃 ​ ( X → ) \mathtt{FC_{r_{j},{r_{i}^{k}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}}}(\vec{X}) as follows. We unify the variables of the k t ​ h k^{th}
goal in the firing rule for r i r_{i} with the head variables of the firing rule for r j r_{j} . All remaining variables of r i r_{i} are renamed to avoid name clashes. We add the unified head of r i r_{i} to the body of r j r_{j} .
These rules check whether rule nodes in the provenance graph are connected to nodes in Match ​ ( ψ ) \textsc{Match}(\psi) .

        r r → g ← 𝚎𝚍𝚐𝚎 ⁡ ( f 𝚛 F ​ ( a ​ r ​ g ​ s ) , f 𝚐 F ​ ( t ) ) : − 𝙵 𝚛 , F ​ ( a ​ r ​ g ​ s ) r_{r\to g}\leftarrow\mathtt{edge}(f_{\mathtt{r}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(args),f_{\mathtt{g}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(t))\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}F}}(args)
     
37:
      P M ← P M : : r r → g P_{M}\leftarrow P_{M}\,{\tt::}\,r_{r\to g}              
38:
   return P M P_{M}
Algorithm 6 Create Edge Relation
8.5 Computing the Edge Relation
The program created so far captures sufficient information for generating the edge
relation of the explanation for a PQ (which is used when rendering graphs). We make this step part of the program to offload this work to database backend.
To compute the edge relation, we use Skolem functions to create node
identifiers.
An identifier records the type of the node (tuple, rule, or
goal), variables assignments, and the success/failure status of the
node, e.g., a tuple node 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{T}(n,s) that is successful would be represented as
f 𝚃 T ​ ( n , s ) f_{\mathtt{T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s) . Each rule firing corresponds to a fragment of 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) . For example, one such fragment is shown in Fig.  11 (left). Such a substructure is created through a set of rules:
•
One rule creating edges between tuple nodes for the head predicate and
rule nodes
•
One rule for each goal connecting a rule node to that goal node (only failed goals for
failed rules)
•
One rule creating edges between each goal node and the corresponding EDB tuple node

Algorithm 7 Create Lineage Edge Relation
8.6 𝒦 {\cal K} -Explanations
To compute one of the 𝒦 {\cal K} -explanation types introduced in Sec.  6.2 , we only have to
adapt the rules generating the edge relation. As an example, we present the modifications for computing Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} (e.g., Fig.  4c ). Recall that semiring 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \mathsf{Which}(X) models provenance as a set of contributing tuples and we encode this as a graph by connecting a head of a rule derivation to the atoms in its body.
That is, for the Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} , we create only one type of rule that connects tuple nodes for the head predicate to EDB tuple nodes.
We use 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}}^{\mathsf{Which}(X)} to denote the program generated in this way for an input program P P , and a PQ ψ \psi .
Example 16 .
Consider the graph fragment for r 1 r_{1} in Fig.  11 (left) without rule and goal nodes.
The rule that creates the edge between 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) and 𝚃 ⁡ ( n , Z ) \mathtt{T}(n,Z) is
𝚎𝚍𝚐𝚎 ⁡ ( f 𝚀 T ​ ( n , s ) , f 𝚃 T ​ ( n , Z ) ) : − 𝙵 𝚛 𝟷 , T ​ ( n , s , Z ) \mathtt{edge}(f_{\mathtt{Q}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s),f_{\mathtt{T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,Z))\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{F}_{\mathtt{r_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.45,0}T}}(n,s,Z)
For each successful derivation of result 𝚀 ⁡ ( n , s ) \mathtt{Q}(n,s) using rule r 1 r_{1} , a subgraph replacing Z Z with bindings from the derivation is included in Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} .
8.7 Correctness
We now prove that our approach is correct.
Theorem 5 .
Let P P be a program, I I be a instance, and ψ \psi a PQ . Program 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}} evaluated over I I returns the edge relation of Expl ​ ( P , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}(P,\psi,I) .
Proof.
To prove Theorem  5 , we have to show that 1) only edges from 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) are in 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ ​ ( I ) \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}}(I) and 2) the program returns precisely the set of edges of
explanation Expl ​ ( P , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}(P,\psi,I) .
1.
The constants used as variable binding by the rules creating edges in 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}} are either constants that occur in the PQ ψ \psi or the result of rules which are evaluated over the instance I I .
Since only the rules for creating the edge relation create new values (through Skolem functions), it follows that any constant used in constructing a node argument exists in the associated domain. Recall that the 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) only contains nodes with arguments from the associated domain. Any edge returned by 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}} is strictly based on the structure of the input program and connects nodes that agree on variable bindings.
Thus, each edge produced by 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}} will be contained in 𝒫𝒢 ⁡ ( P , I ) {\cal PG}(P,I) .
2.
We now prove that the program 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}} returns precisely the set of edges of Expl ​ ( P , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}(P,\psi,I) .
Assume that the PQ ψ \psi only uses constants (the extension to PQ s which contain variables is immediate).
Consider a rule of an input program of depth 1 1 (i.e., only EDB predicates in the rule body). For such a rule node to be connected to an atom Q ​ ( t ) ∈ Match ​ ( ψ ) Q(t)\in\textsc{Match}(\psi) , its head variables have to be bound to t t (guaranteed by the unification step in Sec.  8.1 ). Since the firing rules are known to be correct, this guarantees that exactly the rule nodes connected to the PQ node are generated. The propagation of this unification to the firing rules for EDB predicates is correct, because only EDB nodes agreeing with this binding can be connected to such a rule node. However, propagating constants is not sufficient since the firing rule for an EDB predicate (e.g., R R ) may return irrelevant tuples, i.e., tuples that are not part of any rule derivations for Q ⁡ ( t ) Q(t) (e.g., there may not exist EDB tuples for other goals in the rule which share variables with the particular goal using predicate R R ). This is checked by the connectivity joins (Sec.  8.4 ). If a tuple is returned by a connected firing rule, then the corresponding node is guaranteed to be connected to at least one rule node deriving PQ .
Note that this argument does not rely on the fact that predicates in the body of a rule are EDB predicates. Thus, we can apply this argument in a proof by induction to show that, given that rules of depth up to n n only produce connected rule derivations, the same holds for rules of depth n + 1 n+1 .
∎
Theorem 6 .
Let P P be a positive program, I I be a database instance, and ψ \psi a PQ . The result of program 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}}^{\mathsf{Which}(X)} is the edge relation of Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) ​ ( P , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)}(P,\psi,I) .
Proof.
We prove Theorem  6 by induction over the structure of a program as in the proof of Theorem  4 .
1) Base Case.
Consider a program P P with depth 1 1 and P P has a single IDB predicate 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} , i.e., only containing rules of the form r i : 𝚀 ⁡ ( X → ) : − 𝚁 𝟷 𝚒 ​ ( X 1 i → ) , … , 𝚁 𝚗 𝚒 𝚒 ​ ( X n i i → ) r_{i}:\mathtt{Q}(\vec{X})\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{R_{1}^{i}}(\vec{X_{1}^{i}}),\ldots,\mathtt{R_{n_{i}}^{i}}(\vec{X_{n_{i}}^{i}}) , where each 𝚁 𝚓 \mathtt{R_{j}} is an EDB relation and all goals are positive.
According to [ 15 ] , the semiring annotation of a tuple 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) in the result of a positive Datalog program is computed as a sum of products. This sum contains one monomial per successful rule derivation with head 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) . Such a monomial is constructed by multiplying the annotations of the grounded goals in the rule derivation. Let ν \nu denote a variable assignment corresponding to a rule derivation and V ​ a ​ l ​ ( r , 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) ) Val(r,\mathtt{Q}(t)) the set of all variable assignment for the rule r r that yield 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) . Since addition and multiplication are idempotent in 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \mathsf{Which}(X) ,
the annotation of a result 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) is computed as below:
⋃ i ⋃ ν ∈ V ​ a ​ l ​ ( r i , 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) ) ⋃ j = 1 n i ν ⁡ ( R j i ​ ( X j i ) ) \bigcup_{i}\hskip 5.69054pt\bigcup_{\nu\in Val(r_{i},\mathtt{Q}(t))}\hskip 5.69054pt\bigcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\nu(R_{j}^{i}(X_{j}^{i}))
That is, the 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \mathsf{Which}(X) expression of 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) contains the set of annotations of all tuples that appear in at least one successful derivation of 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) . In Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} , the fact that a tuple 𝚁 ⁡ ( t ′ ) \mathtt{R}(t^{\prime}) belongs to the provenance of 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) is recorded as an edge from 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) to 𝚁 ⁡ ( t ′ ) \mathtt{R}(t^{\prime}) . Thus, to prove that the generated Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} graph correctly encodes 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \mathsf{Which}(X) , we have to show that such an edge exists for every goal of a successful rule derivation with 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) as the head.
In Theorem 4 , we have proven that firing rules correctly determine existence of tuples and successful/failed derivations for the user’s provenance question. The adapted algorithm creates one rule for every goal of a rule which returns an edge if the goal is part of a successful rule derivation (this is ensured by using firing rules).
Thus, an edge exists for every goal of a successful derivation of 𝚀 ⁡ ( t ) \mathtt{Q}(t) .
2) Inductive Step.
Assume that the algorithm is correct for any program of depth less than n n .
Consider the program P P with depth n n and a derivation of a rule r r of depth n n in this program.
Based on the induction hypothesis, we know that the 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \mathsf{Which}(X) annotation for each atom in the body of the derivation is recorded correctly.
From Theorem 4 and using the same argument as in the base case, the rules created for rule r r will generate edges that link the result tuple of rule r r to each atom in its body (the claim holds).
∎
r 3 : 𝚀 𝟸 ​ 𝚑 ​ 𝚘 ​ 𝚙 ​ ( X ) : − 𝙷 ⁡ ( Y , L 1 , Z ) , 𝙷 ⁡ ( Z , L 2 , X ) \displaystyle r_{3}:\mathtt{Q_{2hop}}(X)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{H}(Y,L_{1},Z),\mathtt{H}(Z,L_{2},X)
