9 Factorization
For provenance polynomials, we can exploit the distributivity law of semirings to generate
factorizations of provenance [ 31 ] which are exponentially more concise in the best case.
For instance, consider a query r 3 r_{3} returning the end points of paths of length 2 evaluated over the edge-labelled graph in Fig.  12a .
The provenance polynomial for the query result 𝚀 𝟸 ​ 𝚑 ​ 𝚘 ​ 𝚙 ​ ( d ) \mathtt{Q_{2hop}}(d) using the annotations from Fig.  12a is shown in Fig.  12d . Each monomial in the polynomial corresponds to one of the derivations of the result using r 3 r_{3} . Each of these 2 ⋅ ( 2 2 ) 2\cdot(2^{2})
(we have two options as starting points and, for each hop, we have two options) derivations corresponds to one path of length 2 2 ending in d d .
When generating provenance graphs for provenance polynomials, we create “ ⋅ \cdot ” nodes for rule derivations and “ + + ” nodes for IDB tuples.
Fig.  12b is the factorized representation of this polynomial. We can exploit the fact that our approach shares common subexpressions to produce a particular factorization.
This is achieved by rewriting the input program to partition a query by materializing joins and projections as new IDB relations which can then be shared.
We first review f-trees and d-trees as introduced in [ 32 ]
which encode possible nesting “schemas” for factorized representations of provenance (or query results), the size bounds for factorized representations based on d-trees proven in [ 32 ] , and how to chose a d-tree for a query that results in the optimal worst-case size bound for the factorized representation of the provenance according to this d-tree.
Then, we introduce a query transformation for conjunctive queries which, given an input query and the d-tree for this query, generates a rewritten query which returns a provenance graph factorized corresponding to this d-tree. We employ this rewriting to produce more concise provenance in PUG (experiments are shown in Sec.  11 ).
Factorized Representations.
In [ 31 , 32 ] , a factorized representation (f-rep for short) of a relation is defined as an algebraic expression constructed using singleton relations (one tuple with one value) and the relational operators union and product. Any f-rep over a set of attributes from a schema S S can be interpreted as a relation over S S by evaluating the algebraic expression, e.g., { ( a ) } × ( { ( b ) } ∪ { ( c ) } ) \{(a)\}\times(\{(b)\}\cup\{(c)\}) is a factorized representation of the relation { ( a , b ) , ( a , c ) } \{(a,b),(a,c)\} . Following the convention from [ 31 ] , we denote a singleton { ( a ) } \{(a)\} as a a .
Factorization can be applied to compactly represent relations and query results as well as provenance (e.g., Fig.  12b ). We will factorize representations of provenance
which encode variables of provenance polynomials as the tuples annotated by these variables and show how to extract provenance polynomials from provenance graphs generated in this way.
F-trees for F-reps.
Olteanu et al. [ 32 ] introduce f-trees to encode the nesting
structure of f-reps. At first, let us consider only f-trees which encode the
nesting structure of a boolean query [ 31 ] . An f-tree for a boolean query Q Q (e.g., r 4 r_{4} in Fig.  12a ) is a rooted
forest with one node for every variable of Q Q . 4 4
4
In [ 32 ] ,
relational algebra is used to express queries and nodes of f-trees represent
equivalence classes of attributes which in Datalog correspond to query
variables. An f-rep according to an f-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} nests values according
to 𝒯 \mathcal{T} : a node labelled with X X corresponds to a union of values from the
attributes bound to X X by the query. The values of attributes bound to children
of a node X X corresponding to a single value x x bound to X X are grouped under
x x . If a node has multiple children, then their f-reps are connected via
× \times . For example, consider an f-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} with root X X and a single
child Y Y for a query 𝚀 ⁡ ( ) : − 𝚁 ⁡ ( X , Y ) \mathtt{Q}()\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{R}(X,Y) . An f-rep
of 𝚀 \mathtt{Q} according to 𝒯 \mathcal{T} would be of the form
x 1 × ( y 1 1 ∪ … ∪ y n 1 ) ∪ … ∪ x m × ( y 1 m ∪ … ∪ y n m ) x_{1}\times(y_{1_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup y_{n_{1}})\cup\ldots\cup x_{m}\times(y_{1_{m}}\cup\ldots\cup y_{n_{m}}) , i.e., the Y Y values co-occurring with a
given X X value x x are grouped as a union and then paired with x x . An f-tree
encodes (conditional) independence of the variables of a query in the sense that the
values of one variable do not depend on the values of another variable. For
instance, two siblings X X and Y Y in an f-tree have to be independent since a
union of X X values is paired (cross-product) with a union of Y Y values. This is only correct if the values of X X and Y Y are independent.
The independence assumptions encoded in an f-tree may not hold for every possible query with the same schema as the f-tree.
Thus, only some f-trees with a particular schema may be applicable for a query with this schema.
It was shown in [ 32 ] , that a query has an f-rep over
an f-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} for any database iff for each relation in Q Q the variables assigned to attributes of this relation
(these variables are called dependent) are on the same root-to-leaf path in the
f-tree. This is called the path condition . Note that multiple references to the
same relation in a query are considered as separate relations when checking this
condition. For instance, consider the boolean query r 4 r_{4} in Fig.  12a which checks if there are paths of length 2
ending in the node d d . Fig.  12c shows two f-trees 𝒯 1 \mathcal{T}_{1}
and 𝒯 2 \mathcal{T}_{2} for this query (ignore the sets on the side of nodes for now). An f-rep according to 𝒯 2 \mathcal{T}_{2} for r 4 r_{4} would
encode a union of Y Y values paired ( × \times ) with a union of Z Z values for this Y Y
value. Each Z Z value nested under a Y Y value is then paired with a
cross-product of L 1 L_{1} and L 2 L_{2} values.
D-trees for D-reps.
The size of a factorized representation can be further reduced by allowing subexpressions to be shared through definitions, i.e., using algebra graphs instead of trees. In [ 32 ] , such representations are called d-representations (d-rep).
Analogous to how f-trees define the
structure of f-reps, d-trees were introduced to define the structure of d-reps.
A d-tree is an f-tree where each node X X is annotated with a set k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) key(X) , a subset of its ancestors in the f-tree on which the node and any of its dependents depend on. The f-rep of the subtree rooted in X X is unique for each combination of values from k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) key(X) . That is, if k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) key(X) is a strict subset of the ancestors of X X , then the same d-rep for the subtree at X X can be shared by multiple ancestors, reducing the size of the representation. In Fig.  12c , the set k ​ e ​ y key is shown beside each node, e.g., in 𝒯 2 \mathcal{T}_{2} , the variable L 2 L_{2} depends only on Z Z , but not on Y Y .
An important result proven in [ 32 ] is that, for a given d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} for a query Q Q , the size of d-rep of Q Q over a database I I is bound by | I | s ↑ ​ ( 𝒯 ) |{I}|^{s^{\uparrow}(\mathcal{T})} where s ↑ ​ ( 𝒯 ) s^{\uparrow}(\mathcal{T}) is a rational number computed based on 𝒯 \mathcal{T} alone (see [ 32 ] for details of how to compute s ↑ ​ ( 𝒯 ) s^{\uparrow}(\mathcal{T}) ). This bound can be used to determine the d-tree for a query Q Q which will yield the d-rep of worst-case optimal size by enumerating the valid d-trees for Q Q and, then, chosing the one with the lowest value of s ↑ s^{\uparrow} .
Example 17 .
Consider the d-rep for r 4 r_{4} (Fig.  12a )
over the example instance of relation 𝙷 \mathtt{H} (Fig.  12a ) according to d-tree 𝒯 2 \mathcal{T}_{2} (Fig.  12c ).
Variable Y Y at the root of 𝒯 2 \mathcal{T}_{2} is bound to the attribute S S from the first reference of 𝙷 \mathtt{H} , i.e., the starting point of paths of length 2 2 ending in d d . There are two such starting points a a and b b . Now each of these are paired with the only valid intermediate node c c on these paths (variable Z Z ). Finally, for this node, we compute the cross-product of the L 1 L_{1} and L 2 L_{2} values connected to c c . Since the L 2 L_{2} values only depend on Z Z , we share these values when the same Z Z value is paired with multiple Y Y values. The final result is ( a × c × ( l 1 ∪ l 2 ) × l ↑ ) + ( b × c × ( l 3 ∪ l 4 ) × l ↑ ) (a\times c\times(l_{1}\cup l_{2})\times l^{\uparrow})+(b\times c\times(l_{3}\cup l_{4})\times l^{\uparrow}) where l ↑ ​ := ⁡ ( l 5 ∪ l 6 ) l^{\uparrow}\defas(l_{5}\cup l_{6}) .
Factorization of Provenance.
For the provenance of a conjunctive query Q Q that is not a boolean query, i.e., it has one or more variables in the head (e.g., r 3 r_{3} in Fig.  12a ), we have to compute a provenance polynomial for each result of Q Q .
We would like the factorization of the provenance of Q Q to clearly associate the provenance polynomial of a result t t with the tuple t t . That is, we want to avoid factorizations where head variables of Q Q are nested below variables that store provenance (appear only in the body) since reconstructing the provenance polynomial for t t would require enumeration of the full provenance from the factorized representation in the worst case. For example, consider a query with head variable
X X and body variable Y Y . If Y Y is the root of a d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} , then the d-rep of Q Q according to 𝒯 \mathcal{T} would be of the form y 1 × ( x 1 1 + … + x n 1 ) + … + y m × ( x 1 m + … + x n m ) y_{1}\times(x_{1_{1}}+\ldots+x_{n_{1}})+\ldots+y_{m}\times(x_{1_{m}}+\ldots+x_{n_{m}}) . To extract the provenance polynomial for a result x i x_{i} , we may have to traverse all y y values since there is no indication, for which y y values, x i x_{i} appears in the sum x 1 i + … + x n i x_{1_{i}}+\ldots+x_{n_{i}} .
We ensure this by constructing d-trees which do not include the head variables, but treat those as ancestors of every node in the d-tree when computing k ​ e ​ y key for the nodes.
For instance, to make 𝒯 1 \mathcal{T}_{1} (Fig.  12c ) a valid d-tree for capturing the provenance of r 3 r_{3} (Fig.  12a ), we treat the head variable X X as a virtual ancestor of all nodes and get k ​ e ​ y ​ ( Z ) = { X } key(Z)=\{X\} and k ​ e ​ y ​ ( L 2 ) = { Z , X } key(L_{2})=\{Z,X\} .
Furthermore, if we are computing an explanation to a provenance question (PQ) ψ \psi that binds one or more head variables to constants, then we can propagate these bindings before constructing a d-tree for the query. For example, to explain 𝚀 𝟸 ​ 𝚑 ​ 𝚘 ​ 𝚙 ​ ( d ) \mathtt{Q_{2hop}}(d) , we would propagate the binding X = d X=d resulting in rule r 4 r_{4} (Fig.  12a ). Thus, any d-tree for r 4 r_{4} can be used to create a factorized Expl ℕ ⁡ [ X ] \textsc{Expl}_{\mathbb{N}[X]} graph for the user question Why ​ ( 𝚀 𝟸 ​ 𝚑 ​ 𝚘 ​ 𝚙 ​ ( d ) ) \textsc{Why}\,(\mathtt{Q_{2hop}}(d)) .
Rewriting Queries for Factorization.
We now explain how, given a d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} for a conjunctive query Q Q and positive PQ ψ ​ := ⁡ Why ​ Q ​ ( t ) \psi\defas\textsc{Why}\,Q(t) , to generate a Datalog query Q r ​ e ​ w ​ r Q_{rewr} such that, for any database I I , we have that Expl ℕ ⁡ [ X ] ​ ( Q r ​ e ​ w ​ r , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathbb{N}[X]}(Q_{rewr},\psi,I) encodes ℕ ​ [ X ] ​ ( Q r ​ e ​ w ​ r , I , t ) \mathbb{N}[X](Q_{rewr},I,t) for each t ∈ Match ​ ( ψ ) t\in\textsc{Match}(\psi) factorized according to 𝒯 \mathcal{T} .
We first unify the query with the PQ as described in Sec.  8.1 .
Given a unified input query Q Q and a d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} , we compute Q r ​ e ​ w ​ r Q_{rewr} as follows.
1.
Assume a total order among the variables of Q Q (e.g., the lexicographical order). For every node X X with children Y 1 Y_{1} , …, Y n Y_{n} in the d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} , we generate
r X : 𝚀 𝚇 ​ ( k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) ) : − 𝚀 𝚈 𝟷 ​ ( k ​ e ​ y ​ ( Y 1 ) ) , … , 𝚀 𝚈 𝚗 ​ ( k ​ e ​ y ​ ( Y n ) ) r_{X}:\mathtt{Q_{X}}(key(X))\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{Q_{Y_{1}}}(key(Y_{1})),\ldots,\mathtt{Q_{Y_{n}}}(key(Y_{n}))
2.
Now for every atom 𝚁 ⁡ ( Z 1 , … , Z m ) \mathtt{R}(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{m}) in the body of Q Q , we find the shortest path starting in a root node that contains all nodes Z 1 Z_{1} to Z m Z_{m} . Let Y = Z i Y=Z_{i} for some i i be the last node on this path. Then, we add atom 𝚁 ⁡ ( Z 1 , … , Z m ) \mathtt{R}(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{m}) to the body of rule r Y r_{Y} created in the previous step.
3.
Let X 1 X_{1} , …, X n X_{n} be the roots of the d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} (being a forest, a d-tree may have multiple roots). Furthermore, let Y 1 Y_{1} , …, Y m Y_{m} denote the head variables of the unified input query Q Q with the PQ. We create
r Q : 𝚀 ⁡ ( Y 1 , … , Y m ) : − 𝚀 𝚇 𝟷 ​ ( k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X 1 ) ) , … , 𝚀 𝚇 𝚗 ​ ( k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X n ) ) r_{Q}:\mathtt{Q}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{Q_{X_{1}}}(key(X_{1})),\ldots,\mathtt{Q_{X_{n}}}(key(X_{n}))
The rewriting above creates a factorization according to a d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} . However, it may contain rules which cannot potentially lead to reuse and, thus, result in overhead that could be avoided if we were able to identify such rules. We now present an optimization that removes such rules to further reduce the size of the generated provenance graphs.
Consider two nodes X X and Y Y in a d-tree where Y Y is the only child of X X , i.e., k ​ e ​ y ​ ( Y ) = k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) ∪ { X } key(Y)=key(X)\cup\{X\} . We would generate rules
r X : 𝚀 𝚇 ​ ( k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) ) : − 𝚀 𝚈 ​ ( X ∪ k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) ) \displaystyle r_{X}:\mathtt{Q_{X}}(key(X))\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{Q_{Y}}(X\cup key(X))
r Y : 𝚀 𝚈 ​ ( X ∪ k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) ) : − … \displaystyle r_{Y}:\mathtt{Q_{Y}}(X\cup key(X))\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\ldots
In this case, the intermediate result Q Y Q_{Y} does not lead to further factorization (we have a union of unions). Thus, we can merge the rules by substituting the atom 𝚀 𝚈 ​ ( X ∪ k ​ e ​ y ​ ( X ) ) \mathtt{Q_{Y}}(X\cup key(X)) in r X r_{X} with the body of r Y r_{Y} . A similar situation may arise with the rule r Q r_{Q} deriving the final query result. In general, we can merge any rule of the form 𝚀 𝟷 ​ ( X 1 , … , X n ) : − 𝚀 𝟸 ​ ( X 1 , … , X n ) \mathtt{Q_{1}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{Q_{2}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}) with the rule deriving 𝚀 𝟸 \mathtt{Q_{2}} (in our translation, there will be exactly one rule with head 𝚀 𝟸 \mathtt{Q_{2}} ).
Example 18 .
Consider the question Why ​ 𝚀 𝟸 ​ 𝚑 ​ 𝚘 ​ 𝚙 ​ ( d ) \textsc{Why}\,\mathtt{Q_{2hop}}(d) over the query r 3 r_{3} from Fig.  12a . Unifying the query with this question yields r 4 r_{4} (below r 3 r_{3} in the same figure). To rewrite the query according to
the d-tree 𝒯 1 \mathcal{T}_{1} from Fig.  12c , we apply the above algorithm to create rules:
r Q 2 ​ h ​ o ​ p : 𝚀 𝟸 ​ 𝚑 ​ 𝚘 ​ 𝚙 ​ ( ) : − 𝚀 𝚉 ​ ( ) \displaystyle r_{Q_{2hop}}:\mathtt{Q_{2hop}}()\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{Q_{Z}()}
r Z : 𝚀 𝚉 ​ ( ) : − 𝚀 𝙻 𝟷 ​ ( Z ) , 𝚀 𝙻 𝟸 ​ ( Z ) \displaystyle r_{Z}:\mathtt{Q_{Z}()}\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{Q_{L_{1}}}(Z),\mathtt{Q_{L_{2}}}(Z)
r L 1 : 𝚀 𝙻 𝟷 ​ ( Z ) : − 𝚀 𝚈 ​ ( Z , L 1 ) \displaystyle r_{L_{1}}:\mathtt{Q_{L_{1}}}(Z)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{Q_{Y}}(Z,L_{1})
r Y : 𝚀 𝚈 ​ ( Z , L 1 ) : − 𝙷 ⁡ ( Y , L 1 , Z ) \displaystyle r_{Y}:\mathtt{Q_{Y}}(Z,L_{1})\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{H}(Y,L_{1},Z)
r L 2 : 𝚀 𝙻 𝟸 ​ ( Z ) : − 𝙷 ⁡ ( Z , L 2 , d ) \displaystyle r_{L_{2}}:\mathtt{Q_{L_{2}}}(Z)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{H}(Z,L_{2},d)
Applying the optimizations introduced above, we merge the rules r Q 2 ​ h ​ o ​ p r_{Q_{2hop}} with r Z r_{Z} (the head 𝚀 Z \mathtt{Q}_{Z} is the body of r Q 2 ​ h ​ o ​ p r_{Q_{2hop}} ). Since k ​ e ​ y ​ ( Y ) = k ​ e ​ y ​ ( L 1 ) ∪ { L 1 } key(Y)=key(L_{1})\cup\{L_{1}\} and L 1 L_{1} has only one child, we merge r Y r_{Y} into r L 1 r_{L_{1}} . The resulting program is shown as rules r 5 r_{5} , r 5 ′ r_{5^{\prime}} and r 5 ′′ r_{5^{\prime\prime}} in Fig.  12a .
Factorized Explanations.
To generate a concise factorization of provenance for a PQ ψ \psi over a conjunctive query Q Q , we first find a d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} with minimal s ↑ s^{\uparrow} among all d-trees for Q Q (such a d-tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} guarantees worst-case optimal size bounds for the generated factorization). Then, we rewrite the input query according to 𝒯 \mathcal{T} (explained above) and use the approach in Sec.  8 to generate Expl ℕ ⁡ [ X ] ​ ( Q r ​ e ​ w ​ r , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathbb{N}[X]}(Q_{rewr},\psi,I) encoding the d-rep of ℕ ​ [ X ] ​ ( Q r ​ e ​ w ​ r , I , t ) \mathbb{N}[X](Q_{rewr},I,t) for each t ∈ Match ​ ( ψ ) t\in\textsc{Match}(\psi) .
Example 19 .
Continuing with Example 18 , assume we compute the ℕ ⁡ [ X ] \mathbb{N}[X] explanation using the rewritten query ( r 5 r_{5} , r 5 ′ r_{5^{\prime}} , and r 5 ′′ r_{5^{\prime\prime}} ). The result over the example database is shown in Fig.  12b . The top-most addition and multiplication correspond to the successful derivation using rule r 5 r_{5} (using c c as an intermediate hop from some node to d d ). The left branch below the multiplication encodes the four possible derivations of 𝚀 𝙻 𝟷 ​ ( c ) \mathtt{Q_{L_{1}}}(c) ( s 1 + s 2 + t 1 + t 2 s_{1}+s_{2}+t_{1}+t_{2} ) and the right branch corresponds to the two derivations of 𝚀 𝙻 𝟸 ​ ( c ) \mathtt{Q_{L_{2}}}(c) ( u 1 + u 2 u_{1}+u_{2} ). The polynomial captured by this graph is ( s 1 + s 2 + t 1 + t 2 ) ⋅ ( u 1 + u 2 ) (s_{1}+s_{2}+t_{1}+t_{2})\cdot(u_{1}+u_{2}) . That is, there are 4 ways to reach c c from any starting node and two ways of reaching d d from c c leading to a total of 4 ⋅ 2 = 8 4\cdot 2=8 paths of length two ending in the node d d .
10 Implementation
We have implemented the approach presented in this paper in a system called PUG (Provenance Unification through Graphs).
PUG is an extension of GProM [ 1 ] , a middleware that executes provenance requests using a relational database backend (shown in Fig.  13 ).
We have extended the system to support Datalog enriched with syntax for stating provenance questions. The user provides a why or why-not question and the corresponding Datalog query as an input.
Our system parses and semantically analyzes this input. Schema
information is gathered by querying the catalog of the backend database (e.g.,
to determine whether an EDB predicate exists). Modules
for accessing schema information are already part of the GProM system, but a new
semantic analysis component had to be developed to support Datalog.
The algorithms presented in
Sec.  8 are applied to create the program
𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}} for the input program P P and the provenance question ψ \psi
which computes Expl ​ ( P , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}(P,\psi,I) (analogously, Expl 𝒦 ​ ( P , ψ , I ) \textsc{Expl}_{{\cal K}}(P,\psi,I) for 𝔾 ​ ℙ P , ψ 𝒦 \mathbb{GP}_{{P},{\psi}}^{{\cal K}} ). This program is then translated into relational algebra ( ℛ𝒜 \cal RA ). The
resulting algebra expression is translated into SQL and sent to the backend database to compute the edge relation of
the explanation for the question. Based on this edge relation, we render a provenance graph. For examples and installation guidelines see: https://github.com/IITDBGroup/PUG .
While it would certainly be possible to directly translate the
Datalog program into SQL without the intermediate translation into ℛ𝒜 \cal RA , we choose to introduce this step to be able to leverage the existing
heuristic and cost-based optimizations for ℛ𝒜 \cal RA expressions provided by
GProM [ 30 ] and use its library of ℛ𝒜 \cal RA to SQL translators.
 
 
Figure 13: PUG implementation in GProM
Our translation of first-order queries (a program with a distinguished answer relation) to ℛ𝒜 \cal RA
is mostly standard. See [ 25 ] for details and an example.
11 Experiments
We evaluate the performance of our solution
over a co-author graph relation extracted from DBLP ( http://www.dblp.org )
as well as over the TPC-H benchmark dataset ( http://www.tpc.org/tpch/default.asp ).
We mainly evaluate three aspects;
1) we compare our approach for computing explanations ( Expl ) with the approach introduced for provenance games [ 24 ] . We call the provenance game approach Direct Method (DM) , because it
directly constructs the full provenance graph; 2) we compare our approach for Lineage ( Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} ) to the language-integrated approach developed for the Links programming language [ 9 ] ; 3) we evaluate the performance impact of rewriting queries to produce factorized provenance (Sec.  9 ).
We have created subsets of the DBLP dataset with 100, 1K, 10K, 100K, 1M,
and 8M co-author pairs (tuples).
For the TPC-H benchmark, we used database sizes 10MB, 100MB, 1GB, and 10GB.
All experiments were run on a machine with 2 x 3.3Ghz AMD Opteron 4238 CPUs (12 cores in total) and 128GB RAM running Oracle Linux 6.4.
We use the commercial DBMS X (name omitted due to licensing restrictions) and Postgres as a backend (DBMS X is the default). Unless stated otherwise, each experiment was repeated 100 times (we stopped executions that ran longer than 10 minutes) and we report the median runtime.
Computations that did
not finish within the allocated time are omitted from the graphs.

Figure 14: DBLP and TPC-H queries for experiments
Workloads.
We compute explanations for the queries in Fig.  14 . For DBLP datasets, we consider: 𝚘𝚗𝚕𝚢𝟸𝚑𝚘𝚙 \mathtt{only2hop} ( r 1 r_{1} ) which is our running example query in this paper;
𝚇𝚠𝚒𝚝𝚑𝚈𝚗𝚘𝚝𝚉 \mathtt{XwithYnotZ} ( r 2 r_{2} ) that returns authors that are direct co-authors of a certain person Y Y ,
but not of “Svein Johannessen”;
𝚘𝚗𝚕𝚢𝟹𝚑𝚘𝚙 \mathtt{only3hop} ( r 3 r_{3} ) that returns pairs of authors ( X , Y ) (X,Y) that are connected via a path of length 3 in the co-author graph where X X is not a co-author or indirect co-author (2 hops) of Y Y . For TPC-H, we consider: 𝚘𝚛𝚍𝙿𝚛𝚒𝚘𝚛𝚒𝚝𝚢 \mathtt{ordPriority} ( r 4 r_{4} ) which returns for each customer the priorities of her/his orders;
𝚘𝚛𝚍𝙳𝚒𝚜𝚌 \mathtt{ordDisc} ( r 5 r_{5} ) which returns customers and the discount rates of items in their orders;
𝚙𝚊𝚛𝚝𝙽𝚘𝚝𝙰𝚜𝚒𝚊 \mathtt{partNotAsia} ( r 6 r_{6} ) which finds parts that can be supplied from a country that is not in Asia;
𝚜𝚞𝚙𝚙𝙲𝚞𝚜𝚝 \mathtt{suppCust} ( r 7 r_{7} ) returns nations having both suppliers and customers.
Implementing DM.
DM has to instantiate a graph with 𝒪 ⁡ ( | 𝑎𝑑𝑜𝑚 ⁡ ( I ) | n ) {\cal O}(|{\mathit{adom}(I)}|^{n}) nodes
where n n is the maximal number of variables in a rule.
We do not have a full implementation of DM , but compute a conservative
lower bound for the runtime of the step constructing the game graph by executing a query ( n n -way cross-product over the active domain).
Note that the actual runtime will be much higher
because 1) several edges are created for each rule binding (we underestimate the number of nodes of the constructed graph) and 2) recursive Datalog queries have to be evaluated over this graph using the well-founded semantics.
The results for different instance sizes and number of variables are shown in
Fig.  15 .
Even for only 2 variables, DM did not finish
for datasets of more than 10K tuples
within the allocated 10 min timeslot.
For queries with more than 4 4 variables, DM did not even finish for the smallest dataset.

Figure 16: Why questions: DBLP (top), TPC-H (bottom)
Why Questions.
The runtime of generating explanations for why questions
over the queries r 1 r_{1} , r 2 r_{2} , r 4 r_{4} , and r 5 r_{5} (Fig.  14 ) is shown in Fig.  16 .
For the evaluation, we consider the effect of
different binding patterns on performance.
Fig.  16c and 16f show which variables are bound by the provenance questions ( PQ s). Fig.  16a and 16b show the runtime for DBLP queries r 1 r_{1} and r 2 r_{2} , respectively.
We also provide the number of rule nodes in the explanation for each binding pattern below the X axis.
If only variable X X is bound ( BindingX ), then the queries determine authors that occur together with the author we have bound to X X
in the query result. For instance, the explanation
for 𝚘𝚗𝚕𝚢𝟸𝚑𝚘𝚙 \mathtt{only2hop}
with BindingX
explains why persons are indirect, but not direct, co-authors of “Tore Risch”.
If both X X and Y Y are bound ( BindingXY ), then the provenance for r 1 r_{1} and r 2 r_{2} is limited to a particular indirect and direct co-author, respectively.
The runtime for generating explanations grows roughly linear in the dataset size
and outperforms DM even for small instances.
Furthermore, Fig.  16d and 16e
(for r 4 r_{4} and r 5 r_{5} , respectively) show that our approach can handle queries with many variables (attributes in TPC-H) where DM times out
even for the smallest dataset we have considered.
Binding one variable ( BindingY ) in queries r 4 r_{4} and r 5 r_{5}
expresses a condition, e.g., Y Y = ‘1-URGENT’ in r 4 r_{4} requires the order priority to be urgent.
If both variables are bound,
then the PQ verifies the existence of orders for a certain customer
(e.g., why “Customer16” has at least one urgent order). Runtimes exhibit the same trend as for the DBLP queries.

(b) Runtime of 𝚇𝚠𝚒𝚝𝚑𝚈𝚗𝚘𝚝𝚉 \mathtt{XwithYnotZ}

Why-not Provenance.
We use queries r 1 r_{1} and r 2 r_{2} from Fig.  14
to evaluate the performance of computing explanations for failed derivations.
When binding all variables in the PQ ( BindingXY ) using the bindings from Fig.  17c , these queries check if a particular set of authors do not appear together in the result.
For instance, for 𝚘𝚗𝚕𝚢𝟸𝚑𝚘𝚙 \mathtt{only2hop} ( r 1 ) (r_{1}) , the query checks why “Tore Risch” is either
not an indirect co-author or
is a direct co-author of “Svein Johannessen”.
The results for queries r 1 r_{1} and r 2 r_{2} (DBLP) are shown in
Fig.  17a and 17b , respectively.
The number of tuples produced by the provenance computation (the number of rule nodes is shown below the X axis)
is quadratic in the database size resulting in a quadratic increase in runtime.
DM only finishes within the allocated time for very small datasets while our approach scales to larger instances.

Figure 18: Why questions for queries with negation
Queries with Negation.
Recall that our approach also handles queries with negation.
We choose
rules r 3 r_{3} (multiple negated goals)
and r 6 r_{6} (one negated goal) from Fig.  14 to evaluate the performance of answering why questions over such queries.
We use the bindings shown in Fig.  18c .
The results for r 3 r_{3} and r 6 r_{6} are shown in
Fig.  18a and 18b , respectively. These results demonstrate that
our approach efficiently computes explanations for such queries.
When increasing the database size, the runtimes of PQs for these queries exhibit the same trend
as observed for other why (why-not) questions and significantly outperform DM .
For instance, the performance of 𝚙𝚊𝚛𝚝𝙽𝚘𝚝𝙰𝚜𝚒𝚊 \mathtt{partNotAsia} (Fig.  18b ),
which contains many variables and negation
exhibits
the same trend as queries that have no negation
(i.e., r 4 r_{4} and r 5 r_{5} in Fig.  16d and Fig.  16e , respectively).
 
Figure 19: Comparing 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \mathsf{Which}(X) in PUG with Links
 
(a) 𝚀𝟽 \mathtt{Q7} (employee)
(b) 𝚀𝙵𝟹 \mathtt{QF3} (employee)
Comparison with Links.
In this experiment, we compare the runtime of computing Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} (e.g., Fig.  4c ) with computation of Lineage in 𝖫𝗂𝗇𝗄𝗌 𝖫 \mathsf{Links^{L}} from [ 9 ] .
We show relative runtimes where PUG is normalized to 1 1 .
For this particular evaluation, we use Postgres as a backend since it is supported by both PUG and Links. Note that Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} contains a full description of each tuple unlike 𝖫𝗂𝗇𝗄𝗌 𝖫 \mathsf{Links^{L}} which returns tuple identifiers (OIDs in Postgres). To get a nuanced understanding of the system’s performance, we show three runtimes for Links: 1) Links is the actual implementation in Links which computes Lineage (only OIDs) and where the runtime includes the construction of in-memory Links types from the provenance fetched from Postgres; 2) LinksQ is the runtime of the queries that Links uses to capture Lineage; and 3) LinksQasEXPL which joins the output of LinksQ with the base tables (i.e., as informative as Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} ).
For LinksQ , the capture the SQL queries that Links runs to capture provenance. We then compare the runtime of these queries to generation of Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} in PUG.
The queries we use for LinksQasEXPL are generated as follows: we join the tuple id and relation
name pairs produced by LinksQ with the corresponding relations in the
database to return full tuples as in PUG’s
Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} provenance type. Recall that PUG encodes Expl 𝖶𝗁𝗂𝖼𝗁 ⁡ ( X ) \textsc{Expl}_{\mathsf{Which}(X)} is
as the edge relation of a DAG of tuple nodes. Therefore, when generating the queries for
LinksQasEXPL , we add code to generate this edge relation from the returned tuples using
string concatenation. For example, one tuple from the edge relation of the graph shown in
Fig.  4c is
( 𝚀 𝟹 ​ 𝚑 ​ 𝚘 ​ 𝚙 ​ ( s , s ) , 𝚃𝚛𝚊𝚒𝚗 ⁡ ( s , s ) ) (\mathtt{Q_{3hop}}(s,s),\mathtt{Train}(s,s)) . 𝖫𝗂𝗇𝗄𝗌 𝖫 \mathsf{Links^{L}}
and LinksQ would represent this part of the provenance as the tuple id of ( s , s ) (s,s) paired with the name of this relation
( 𝚃𝚛𝚊𝚒𝚗 \mathtt{Train} ). For instance, assuming the id of this tuple is 123 123 , we would get ( 123 , 𝚃𝚛𝚊𝚒𝚗 ) (123,\mathtt{Train}) . Based on this information,
LinksQasEXPL computes the edge ( 𝚀 𝟹 ​ 𝚑 ​ 𝚘 ​ 𝚙 ​ ( s , s ) , 𝚃𝚛𝚊𝚒𝚗 ⁡ ( s , s ) ) (\mathtt{Q_{3hop}}(s,s),\mathtt{Train}(s,s)) by
joining with relation 𝚃𝚛𝚊𝚒𝚗 \mathtt{Train} using the tuple-id 123 123 and by applying string concatenation. For example, using || to denote string concatenation,
𝚃𝚛𝚊𝚒𝚗 ⁡ ( s , s ) \mathtt{Train}(s,s) is generated by evaluating the expression
𝚃𝚛𝚊𝚒𝚗 \mathtt{Train} || ’(’ || ’ s ’ || ’,’ || ’ s ’ || ’)’
 
Figure 20: Explanations vs. factorized explanations
 
(a) Runtime of 𝚜𝚞𝚙𝚙𝙲𝚞𝚜𝚝 \mathtt{suppCust}
(b) Runtime of 𝚘𝚛𝚍𝙳𝚒𝚜𝚌 \mathtt{ordDisc}
We choose two queries from [ 9 ] . The query Q7 applies a range condition to the result of a two-way join. QF3 is a self-join on equality with an additional inequality condition (see [ 9 ] for more details). The queries are expressed over two tables 𝚍𝚎𝚙𝚝 \mathtt{dept} and 𝚎𝚖𝚙 \mathtt{emp} . The number of departments is varied from 4 4 to 2048 2048 (by powers of 2 2 to replicate the setting from [ 9 ] ),
and each department has 100 100 employees on average. The relation 𝚍𝚎𝚙𝚝 \mathtt{dept} consists of one attribute (department name), and 𝚎𝚖𝚙 \mathtt{emp} has three attribute (department name, employee name, and salary).
QF3 can be written in Datalog as:
𝚀𝙵𝟹 ⁡ ( N ​ 1 , N ​ 2 ) : − 𝚎𝚖𝚙 ⁡ ( _ , D , N ​ 1 , S ) , 𝚎𝚖𝚙 ⁡ ( _ , D , N ​ 2 , S ) , N ​ 1 ≠ N ​ 2 \displaystyle\mathtt{QF3}(N1,N2)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{emp}(\_,D,N1,S),\mathtt{emp}(\_,D,N2,S),N1\neq N2
The runtimes of queries Q7 and QF3 are shown in Fig.  19a and 19b , respectively.
Links performs better on smaller instances. The gap between Links and PUG shrinks with increasing dataset size. PUG outperforms Links and LinksQasEXPL on larger datasets.
Factorized Explanations.
We now compare the performance of generating provenance for a query ( EXPL ) and a factorized representation of provenance ( Fact ) by rewriting the input query (Sec.  9 ). Factorization techniques perform best for many-to-many joins
(e.g., the query r 7 r_{7} in Fig.  14 ). The rewritten version of 𝚜𝚞𝚙𝚙𝙲𝚞𝚜𝚝 \mathtt{suppCust} ( r 7 ) (r_{7}) producing factorized provenance is shown below.
r 8 : 𝚜𝚞𝚙𝚙𝙲𝚞𝚜𝚝 ⁡ ( N ) : − 𝚜𝚞𝚙𝚙 ⁡ ( N ) , 𝚌𝚞𝚜𝚝 ⁡ ( N ) \displaystyle r_{8}:\mathtt{suppCust}(N)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{supp}(N),\mathtt{cust}(N)
r 8 ′ : 𝚜𝚞𝚙𝚙 ⁡ ( N ) : − 𝚂𝚄𝙿𝙿𝙻𝙸𝙴𝚁 ⁡ ( A , B , C , N , D , E , F ) \displaystyle r_{8^{\prime}}:\mathtt{supp}(N)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{SUPPLIER}(A,B,C,N,D,E,F)
r 8 ′′ : 𝚌𝚞𝚜𝚝 ⁡ ( N ) : − 𝙲𝚄𝚂𝚃𝙾𝙼𝙴𝚁 ⁡ ( G , H , I , N , J , K , L , M ) \displaystyle r_{8^{\prime\prime}}:\mathtt{cust}(N)\,\mathtt{{:}-}\,\mathtt{CUSTOMER}(G,H,I,N,J,K,L,M)
For this experiments, we use a 15 15 minute time-out. The runtimes for r 7 r_{7} (yellow bars) and r 8 r_{8} (red bars) are shown in Fig.  20a .
We show the total result size in bytes below the X X axis.
The runtime of Fact grows roughly linear
unlike EXPL whose growth is quadratic in dataset size. We also evaluate query r 5 r_{5} which includes one-to-many joins to see how Fact performs for a query (Fig.  20b ) where factorization only reduces size by a constant factor.
This is confirmed by the measurements: the performance of Fact for r 5 r_{5} is ∼ 30 % \sim 30\% that of EXPL independent of dataset size.
12 Conclusions
We present a provenance model and unified framework for explaining answers and non-answers over first-order queries expressed in Datalog. Our efficient middleware implementation generates a Datalog program that
computes the explanation
for a provenance question and compiles this program
into SQL. We prove that our model is expressive enough to encode a wide range of provenance models from the literature and extend our approach to produce concise, factorized representations of provenance.
In future work, we will investigate summarization of provenance (we did present a proof-of-concept in [ 26 ] ) to deal with the large size of explanations for missing answers. We plan to also support query-based explanations [ 2 , 3 , 4 , 38 ] and more expressive query languages (e.g., aggregation).

Instructions for reporting errors
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
methods listed below:
Click the "Report Issue" ( ) button, located in the page header.
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
Our team has already identified the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
all.
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion , and welcome developer contributions .
We gratefully acknowledge support from our major funders ,
member institutions , ,
and all contributors.
